---
**Overview**
Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva was the elected president of Brazil in 2023, heading the federal executive after narrowly defeating Jair Bolsonaro in 2022. His authority was real but constrained: he governed with a fragmented Congress, needed centrist and right-leaning votes for major legislation, and faced an assertive judiciary, a politically volatile security environment after the January 8 attacks, and an inherited military establishment still being re-normalized after the Bolsonaro years.

**Evidence Records**

1. **Presidency with constrained governing power**
- **Fact:** Lula held the presidency and could direct the federal executive, but he did not have free hands over the legislature or coercive institutions. Reuters reported him saying the government “needs Congress” and that the left had at most 136 votes, far short of the 257 needed for ordinary approval; a Reuters account also described a large congressional bloc led by Arthur Lira as a force the government had to bargain with.  
- **Why it matters:** This defines the actual scope of Lula’s influence in 2023: executive agenda-setting, but dependence on coalition management and bargaining.
- **Strongest source:** Reuters via UOL, 2 June 2023, “Lula defende diálogo com oposição diante de minoria da esquerda no Congresso.” [Link](https://economia.uol.com.br/noticias/reuters/2023/06/02/lula-diz-que-e-preciso-conversar-com-que-nao-gosta-do-governo-porque-esquerda-e-minoria-no-congresso.htm)
- **Locator:** Reuters quote on congressional math and need to dialogue with opposition.
- **Period:** Requested period.
- **Connection to ruler:** Direct, within Lula’s own authority.
- **Complicating evidence:** The same year Lula’s allies also won key congressional leadership votes, which improved his capacity to govern but did not eliminate dependence on bargaining.
- **Cautions:** This is a political statement to journalists, not a formal institutional measurement.
- **Independent support:** Reuters on the Senate/Camber leadership vote backing Lula, and the Freedom House report on a polarized political arena.
- **Retrospective note:** None.

2. **January 8, 2023 attack on the core institutions**
- **Fact:** On January 8, 2023, supporters of Bolsonaro stormed and vandalized the presidential palace, Congress, and the Supreme Court. Lula, newly in office, responded with a federal intervention in the security of the Federal District, and the STF ordered the removal of camps near military facilities.  
- **Why it matters:** This was the central security and legitimacy shock of Lula’s first weeks and a major limit on presidential control.
- **Strongest source:** Reuters via UOL, 8 January 2023, “Bolsonaristas invadem e vandalizam Planalto, Congresso e STF; Lula decreta intervenção no DF.” [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/01/08/bolsonaristas-rompem-bloqueio-e-invadem-area-da-esplanada.htm)
- **Locator:** Opening paragraphs describing the invasion and Lula’s intervention.
- **Period:** Requested period.
- **Connection to ruler:** Direct, but the event was driven by anti-Lula actors rather than Lula decisions.
- **Complicating evidence:** The attack originated in Bolsonaro-aligned mobilization and police/security failures, so it is evidence of inherited instability as much as Lula’s own governing performance.
- **Cautions:** Early breaking-news reporting; later legal and investigative findings may refine details.
- **Independent support:** CPJ’s January 2023 report on attacks on journalists during the riot; Freedom House on antidemocratic violence in the capital.
- **Retrospective note:** None.

3. **Civil-military tensions and Lula’s reassertion of control**
- **Fact:** Lula publicly rejected the idea that the armed forces were a “moderating power,” said some police and military personnel had been complicit in the January 8 attacks, and replaced the army commander within weeks amid a “fracture of trust.”  
- **Why it matters:** This shows both the military’s political significance and Lula’s effort to restore civilian supremacy.
- **Strongest source:** Reuters via UOL, 12 January 2023, “Lula mostra desconfiança com Forças Armadas e diz que militares não são ‘poder moderador’.” [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/01/12/lula-mostra-desconfanca-com-forcas-armadas-e-diz-que-militares-nao-sao-poder-moderador.htm)
- **Locator:** Lula’s quoted remarks on the military role.
- **Period:** Requested period.
- **Connection to ruler:** Direct.
- **Complicating evidence:** Lula later emphasized coexistence and denied lasting rancor with the army, indicating managed rather than open conflict.
- **Cautions:** Political rhetoric in a crisis context.
- **Independent support:** Reuters report on the army commander replacement, 21 January 2023. [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/01/21/presidente-lula-demite-comandante-do-exercito-julio-cesar-de-arruda-diz-globonews.htm)
- **Retrospective note:** None.

4. **Yanomami humanitarian crisis and state response**
- **Fact:** Lula’s government declared a medical/emergency response in Yanomami territory after reports of child deaths, malnutrition, disease, and illegal mining pressure; the state then launched raids and expulsions of garimpeiros. Later reporting in 2024 said the crisis had not been fully resolved and that 2023 still saw severe mortality.  
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest major domestic governance test involving health, indigenous rights, environmental enforcement, and coercive capacity.
- **Strongest source:** Reuters via Investing, 22 January 2023, “Brazil declares emergency over deaths of Yanomami children from malnutrition.” [Link](https://www.investing.com/news/commodities-news/brazil-declares-emergency-over-deaths-of-yanomami-children-from-malnutrition-2985372)
- **Locator:** Article opening on the health emergency declaration.
- **Period:** Requested period.
- **Connection to ruler:** Direct; the emergency response was a Lula-government decision.
- **Complicating evidence:** A Reuters report from late 2023 / early 2024 indicated illegal miners were returning and the crisis remained severe, suggesting partial rather than durable success.
- **Cautions:** Initial emergency coverage often relies on official statements and field reports; mortality and access figures are hard to verify quickly.
- **Independent support:** UN Brazil annual report noting 13 UN agencies supported the official Yanomami response; UOL/Reuters on intensified removals of invaders. [UN link](https://brasil.un.org/pt-br/271918-relat%C3%B3rio-anual-das-na%C3%A7%C3%B5es-unidas-no-brasil-2023), [Reuters/UOL link](https://economia.uol.com.br/noticias/reuters/2023/04/30/brasil-intensificara-remocao-de-invasores-de-terras-indigenas-apos-assassinato-de-yanomami.htm)
- **Retrospective note:** The later 2024 Reuters/Hutukara report is retrospective evidence about 2023 outcomes.

5. **Economic stabilization: growth, but not uniform welfare gains**
- **Fact:** Brazil’s economy grew in 2023, and later IBGE release data showed GDP up 3.2% to R$10.9 trillion. Separate IBGE poverty data later showed poverty and extreme poverty fell to the lowest levels since 2012.  
- **Why it matters:** Lula’s political program in 2023 emphasized growth, social recovery, and state capacity; the macro and poverty data suggest real progress, though not necessarily evenly distributed.
- **Strongest source:** IBGE, 6 November 2025 release on 2023 GDP. [Link](https://agenciadenoticias.ibge.gov.br/agencia-sala-de-imprensa/2013-agencia-de-noticias/releases/44973-pib-cresce-3-2-e-totaliza-r-10-9-trilhoes-em-2023)
- **Locator:** Highlights section with the 3.2% GDP growth figure.
- **Period:** Later retrospective finding about 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Indirect but substantial; reflects the economic environment under Lula’s government, not a ruler-only causal claim.
- **Complicating evidence:** GDP growth mixes real activity with inflation and composition effects; it does not by itself prove broad welfare improvement.
- **Cautions:** This release is retrospective and aggregated; causation cannot be assigned solely to Lula.
- **Independent support:** IBGE’s February 2024 per-capita income release and December 2024 poverty release. [Income link](https://agenciadenoticias.ibge.gov.br/agencia-sala-de-imprensa/2013-agencia-de-noticias/releases/39262-ibge-divulga-rendimento-domiciliar-per-capita-2023-para-brasil-e-unidades-da-federacao), [Poverty link](https://agenciadenoticias.ibge.gov.br/en/agencia-news/2184-news-agency/news/42113-in-2023-poverty-in-the-country-drops-to-lowest-level-since-2012)
- **Retrospective note:** Yes.

6. **Fiscal compromise and tax reform**
- **Fact:** Lula signed the 2023 budget with vetoes and pushed a fiscal framework and tax reform through Congress, treating the tax reform as a major victory even though Reuters said the final text was the “possible” version rather than his preferred one.  
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest evidence of Lula’s program being constrained by congressional bargaining rather than simply enacted by presidential fiat.
- **Strongest source:** Reuters via UOL, 17 January 2023, “Lula sanciona Orçamento de 2023 com vetos de R$4,3 bi em despesas propostas.” [Link](https://economia.uol.com.br/noticias/reuters/2023/01/17/lula-sanciona-orcamento-de-2023-com-vetos-de-r43-bi-em-despesas-propostas.htm)
- **Locator:** Budget veto reporting and fiscal restraint context.
- **Period:** Requested period.
- **Connection to ruler:** Direct.
- **Complicating evidence:** Reuters later reported Lula saying the approved tax-reform text was not what Haddad wanted, only what was politically possible.
- **Cautions:** This is contemporary political reporting, not a full fiscal evaluation.
- **Independent support:** Reuters on congressional bloc politics and the Senate leadership vote backing Lula.
- **Retrospective note:** None.

7. **Civil liberties and information environment were contested, not fully free**
- **Fact:** Freedom House rated Brazil “Partly Free” in its 2023 internet freedom report and said internet freedom declined slightly amid election-related disinformation, January 8 violence, and aggressive court action against false electoral content.  
- **Why it matters:** It shows that Lula governed in an environment of high polarization, judicial intervention, and contested information flows.
- **Strongest source:** Freedom House, “Brazil: Freedom on the Net 2023 Country Report.” [Link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/brazil/freedom-net/2023)
- **Locator:** Lines 31-35 and 38-41 from the report overview.
- **Period:** Coverage period ends May 31, 2023, but directly relevant to Lula’s first months.
- **Connection to ruler:** Indirect but material, because the government had to operate amid a polarized and heavily litigated information space.
- **Complicating evidence:** Courts’ content-removal actions were defended by some as democracy-protecting rather than censorious.
- **Cautions:** Freedom House is an evaluative index, so it should be treated as context, not as a sole factual source for specific events.
- **Independent support:** Abraji and CPJ reporting on attacks against journalists in early 2023.
- **Retrospective note:** None.

8. **Journalists faced violence and harassment, especially around January 8**
- **Fact:** CPJ reported at least 40 journalists targeted around the Brasília riots and aftermath; Abraji later said 330 attacks on journalists, outlets, or the press were recorded in 2023, with more than 40 cases in January alone after the January 8 attacks.  
- **Why it matters:** Reliable information was harder to obtain because reporters themselves were targeted, intimidated, and physically attacked.
- **Strongest source:** CPJ, 13 January 2023, “At least 40 journalists targeted amid Brazil capital riot and aftermath.” [Link](https://cpj.org/2023/01/at-least-40-journalists-targeted-amid-brazil-capital-riot-and-aftermath/)
- **Locator:** CPJ summary of Abraji/SJPDF counts.
- **Period:** Requested period.
- **Connection to ruler:** Indirect; this reflects the post-Bolsonaro media environment Lula inherited, but it affected his first year of rule.
- **Complicating evidence:** Abraji’s broader 2023 monitoring suggests violence fell from 2022 overall, so the January spike should not be generalized to the full year.
- **Cautions:** Counts are based on monitoring methods and may undercount hidden intimidation.
- **Independent support:** Abraji’s 2023 monitoring report and Freedom House’s discussion of hostility and judicial restrictions. [Abraji link](https://www.abraji.org.br/noticias/monitoramento-abraji-violencia-contra-a-imprensa-cai-40-7-em-2023), [Freedom House link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/brazil/freedom-net/2023)
- **Retrospective note:** Abraji’s year-end summary is retrospective about 2023.

**Information Environment**
- Brazil in 2023 was highly polarized after the 2022 election and the January 8 attacks.
- Courts were unusually active in ordering content removal and account restrictions tied to electoral disinformation.
- Journalists and civil society actors faced harassment and violence, especially in January.
- Official socioeconomic statistics were available, but some of the strongest welfare figures arrived later as retrospective releases.
- Indigenous crisis reporting, especially on Yanomami territory, was strong but still imperfect because access, violence, and illegal mining made field verification hard.

**Questions For Deeper Research**
- How much of Lula’s 2023 legislative success came from his own coalition management versus concessions to centrist blocs?
- How durable was the Yanomami crackdown by year-end, and how much of the crisis was inherited versus caused by 2023 choices?
- Did Lula’s government improve public safety, or mainly react to inherited violence and organized crime risks?
- Were judicial restrictions on disinformation proportionate and legally stable, or did they create new civil-liberties concerns?
- How much did Lula personally shape foreign policy in 2023 beyond rhetorical positioning on Gaza, BRICS, and regional diplomacy?
- Which 2023 social gains were due to Lula’s policy changes versus post-pandemic macro recovery?

- [Reuters via UOL on Congress dependence](https://economia.uol.com.br/noticias/reuters/2023/06/02/lula-diz-que-e-preciso-conversar-com-que-nao-gosta-do-governo-porque-esquerda-e-minoria-no-congresso.htm)

- [Reuters via UOL on January 8](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/01/08/bolsonaristas-rompem-bloqueio-e-invadem-area-da-esplanada.htm)

**Promising Leads Still Needing Inspection**
- UN human-rights reporting on Brazil’s indigenous-rights and police-violence situation.
- Direct official materials on the fiscal framework and 2023 tax-reform bill.
- Official records on Yanomami enforcement operations and their year-end results.
- A direct source on Lula’s 2023 Gaza diplomacy and regional foreign-policy posture.
- Any direct court or TSE material on election-disinformation restrictions if this period needs more legal precision.

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

**Orientation**
Lula was president in 2023, but Brazil’s nuclear and catastrophic-risk posture was largely inherited and institutionally constrained. Brazil had no nuclear weapons program, remained inside the NPT/ABACC safeguards regime, and used nuclear policy for peaceful energy and defense-industrial purposes, not nuclear deterrence. The strongest 2023 changes were in cyber governance, arms control inside the country, continued support for safeguards and disarmament norms, and continued investment in the nuclear-powered submarine program. The main limits were constitutional peaceful-use rules, ABACC/IAEA safeguards, the legislature, the armed forces as a legacy institution, and the practical fact that Brazil’s nuclear posture is not one of independent weapons command.

**Compact evidence index**
1. Lula signed a national cyber-security decree in December 2023, creating a formal policy and committee.
2. Lula reversed Bolsonaro-era arms liberalization in January and July 2023, tightening civilian access and shifting inspection to the Federal Police.
3. Brazil’s nuclear program remained explicitly peaceful, safeguarded, and dual-inspected by ABACC and the IAEA.
4. Brazil continued the submarine-propulsion program, including funding for the nuclear-propulsion line.
5. Lula’s public rhetoric in 2023 opposed war escalation and nuclear catastrophe, but mostly in the context of multilateral diplomacy rather than operational crisis management.
6. The information environment was good on formal acts and official policy, but weaker on operational nuclear-risk claims because there was no weapons program, and because some claims depend on official self-description.

**Lens disposition**
- **1B.1 Reducing existential risk:** Partial evidence. Strong on cyber policy, arms restriction, and peaceful nuclear posture; weak on broader catastrophic-risk architecture beyond cyber and nuclear.
- **1B.10 Lasting risk posture:** Partial evidence. Posture looks safer on civilian weapons and cyber governance; nuclear posture remained peaceful and safeguarded, but the submarine and cyber systems are long-horizon, not fully demonstrated outcomes.
- **1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** Supported. Lula’s 2023 rhetoric and Brazil’s diplomatic texts consistently favored disarmament, peaceful use, and anti-escalation.
- **1B.3 Safe nuclear control:** Supported for peaceful nuclear custody and safeguards, not for nuclear weapons C2 because Brazil had none.
- **1B.4 Arms control and inspections:** Supported. Brazil kept NPT/CTBT-style nonproliferation commitments and ABACC/IAEA safeguards activity.
- **1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression:** Supported as negative evidence. No credible evidence that Lula used nuclear capability to shield aggression or repression.
- **1B.6 Preventing proliferation:** Partially supported. Brazil’s system blocked proliferation at the state level, but this is mainly inherited institutional architecture.
- **1B.7 Independent risk expertise:** Limited evidence. ABACC, CNEN, and GSI structures were active; insufficient evidence of personnel reform specific to catastrophe-risk expertise.
- **1B.8 Crisis de-escalation:** Limited evidence. Lula’s public rhetoric in crises was de-escalatory, but there was no nuclear crisis to test actual command behavior.
- **1B.9 Other catastrophic risks:** Supported on cyber policy only; little evidence on AI, biosecurity, or comparable dual-use domains in 2023.

**Evidence records**

1. **Cyber policy created**
- **Claim:** Lula signed Decree 11.856/2023 creating Brazil’s National Cybersecurity Policy and National Cybersecurity Committee. This matters because it is the clearest 2023 formal act aimed at reducing a major modern catastrophic-risk channel, especially for critical infrastructure.
- **Source:** *Presidente Lula assina decreto que institui a Política Nacional de Cibersegurança*, GSI, 27 Dec 2023. [Link](https://www.gov.br/gsi/pt-br/centrais-de-conteudo/noticias/2023-1/presidente-lula-assina-decreto-que-institui-a-politica-nacional-de-ciberseguranca)
- **Locator:** Lines 261-262 in the opened page; decree reference also in the official text.
- **Period fit:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential signature and executive issuance.
- **Contrary evidence:** This is a framework decree, not proof of implemented resilience improvements.
- **Cautions:** Official self-description; no independent performance evaluation.
- **Corroboration:** Official decree text and ANATEL/GSI policy pages. [Decree](https://www.presidencia.gov.br/ccivil_03/_ato2023-2026/2023/decreto/d11856.htm), [GSI policy page](https://www.gov.br/gsi/pt-br/seguranca-da-informacao-e-cibernetica/PNCiber)

2. **Arms liberalization reversed**
- **Claim:** Lula’s first-day decrees suspended new CAC registrations, restricted access to firearms and ammunition, and began re-regulating civilian gun ownership. This matters because reducing civilian weapon access is relevant to domestic catastrophic violence and escalation risk.
- **Source:** *Lula assina medidas para reverter decretos de armas e sigilos de Bolsonaro*, Reuters via UOL, 1 Jan 2023. [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/01/01/lula-assina-medidas-para-reverter-decretos-de-armas-e-sigilos-de-bolsonaro.htm)
- **Locator:** Opening paragraphs and the bullet list of decree effects.
- **Period fit:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential action.
- **Contrary evidence:** Later 2023 review and implementation details show Brazil still had a large stock of privately held firearms; the decree reduced access but did not remove legacy weapons.
- **Cautions:** Reuters report summarizes an evolving legal package.
- **Corroboration:** Official decree text, July 2023 Reuters report, and the Planalto legal text. [Official legal text](https://www.planalto.gov.br/ccivil_03/_ato2023-2026/2023/decreto/d11615.htm)

3. **Arms control shifted to Federal Police**
- **Claim:** The July 2023 decree sharply tightened civilian firearms rules and transferred inspection/fiscalization from the Army to the Federal Police. This matters because it directly reduced military-adjacent control over civilian weapons.
- **Source:** *Governo Lula edita decreto que restringe acesso a armas e passa fiscalização para a PF*, Reuters via UOL, 21 Jul 2023. [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/07/21/governo-lula-edita-decreto-que-restringe-acesso-a-armas-e-passa-fiscalizacao-para-a-pf.htm)
- **Locator:** Middle paragraphs on reduced access, registration, and transfer of oversight.
- **Period fit:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct executive regulation.
- **Contrary evidence:** The military retained a role in the wholesale control of certain products and the transition took time.
- **Cautions:** Contemporary political reporting; some implementation details unfolded after publication.
- **Corroboration:** Official decree text. [Planalto decree](https://www.planalto.gov.br/ccivil_03/_ato2023-2026/2023/decreto/d11615.htm)

4. **Brazil kept peaceful nuclear doctrine and safeguards**
- **Claim:** Official Brazilian defense material states that nuclear use is exclusively peaceful and that the submarine program’s nuclear propulsion is permitted under the IAEA arrangement, with weapons remaining conventional. This matters because it defines Brazil’s baseline nuclear-risk posture under Lula: no weapons program, but a significant dual-use naval nuclear program.
- **Source:** *Setor nuclear*, Ministry of Defense. [Link](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/assuntos/industria-de-defesa/ciencia-e-tecnologia/setores-estrategicos/setor-nuclear)
- **Locator:** Paragraphs stating peaceful use, IAEA permission, and conventional armaments.
- **Period fit:** Inherited baseline continuing through 2023.
- **Attribution basis:** Institutional baseline, not uniquely Lula-created.
- **Contrary evidence:** The existence of a submarine propulsion program creates dual-use sensitivity and long-term proliferation concern, even without a weapons program.
- **Cautions:** Official self-description; should be paired with ABACC/IAEA reporting.
- **Corroboration:** ABACC liaison committee report and CNEN safeguards pages. [ABACC](https://www.abacc.org.br/en/news/21st-meeting-liaison-committee-lc-quadripartite-safeguards-agreement/), [CNEN safeguards](https://www.gov.br/cnen/pt-br/acesso-a-informacao/transparencia-e-prestacao-de-contas/radioprotecao-e-seguranca-nuclear)

5. **ABACC/IAEA safeguards remained active**
- **Claim:** In October 2023, ABACC said the liaison committee reviewed inspection results from 2022 and discussed safeguards implementation challenges from the expansion of nuclear installations. This matters because it shows the institutional control framework was functioning, while also acknowledging more complex future safeguarding burdens.
- **Source:** *21st Meeting of the Liaison Committee (LC) of the Quadripartite Safeguards Agreement*, ABACC, 9 Oct 2023. [Link](https://www.abacc.org.br/en/news/21st-meeting-liaison-committee-lc-quadripartite-safeguards-agreement/)
- **Locator:** Lines 63-64.
- **Period fit:** Target period, with reference to 2022 inspection results.
- **Attribution basis:** Indirect institutional context.
- **Contrary evidence:** This is not proof of exceptional safety, only active verification.
- **Cautions:** ABACC is part of the safeguards system, so it is not independent of the regime it describes.
- **Corroboration:** CNEN regulatory material and Ministry of Defense nuclear page.

6. **Submarine-propulsion nuclear program retained priority**
- **Claim:** In March 2023 Lula publicly endorsed continued progress on the nuclear-propulsion submarine at Itaguaí and said he expected the program to move forward. This matters because the program is Brazil’s most significant long-term nuclear-adjacent defense project.
- **Source:** *Fala do presidente da República, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, em entrevista coletiva após visita ao Complexo Naval do Itaguaí*, Planalto, interview after 23 Mar 2023 visit. [Link](https://www.gov.br/planalto/pt-br/acompanhe-o-planalto/entrevistas/fala-do-presidente-da-republica-luiz-inacio-lula-da-silva-em-entrevista-coletiva-apos-visita-ao-complexo-naval-do-itaguai)
- **Locator:** Journalist question and Lula’s answer about the “submarino com propulsão nuclear.”
- **Period fit:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential statement.
- **Contrary evidence:** 2023 Reuters/DW reporting still described delays and funding problems, so the program advanced but remained late.
- **Cautions:** A press Q&A is rhetorical and aspirational, not a performance audit.
- **Corroboration:** Novo PAC defense page showing funding line for the nuclear-propulsion submarine. [Defense PAC](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/ajuste/com-novo-pac-defesa-investira-r-53-bilhoes-em-tecnologias-estrategicas-que-garantem-a-soberania-nacional)

7. **Submarine program funded**
- **Claim:** The Novo PAC allocated R$664 million from 2023 to 2026 for the nuclear-propulsion submarine, and R$53 billion total for defense projects. This matters because resources are a key indicator of real priority, not just rhetoric.
- **Source:** *Com novo PAC, Defesa investirá R$ 53 bilhões em tecnologias estratégicas que garantem a soberania nacional*, Ministry of Defense, 11 Aug 2023. [Link](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/ajuste/com-novo-pac-defesa-investira-r-53-bilhoes-em-tecnologias-estrategicas-que-garantem-a-soberania-nacional/)
- **Locator:** Paragraph on “submarino de propulsão nuclear” and the R$664 million figure.
- **Period fit:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Government budget choice.
- **Contrary evidence:** Funding does not prove execution quality or risk reduction.
- **Cautions:** Official program brochure, not an audit.
- **Corroboration:** Casa Civil PAC page. [PAC innovation page](https://www.gov.br/casacivil/pt-br/novopac-antiga/novopac-backup-8-07-2024/inovacao-para-a-industria-da-defesa)

8. **Brazil’s diplomacy remained anti-proliferation oriented**
- **Claim:** At the August 2023 NPT PrepCom, Brazil argued that the treaty’s disarmament and nonproliferation pillars should not be treated as false equivalents and emphasized that disarmament obligations are being underfulfilled. This matters because it shows Brazil was publicly aligned with arms-control norms, not nuclear brinkmanship.
- **Source:** *Intervenção do Brasil (em inglês) no debate sob o Pilar II- Não-Proliferação...*, MRE, 7 Aug 2023. [Link](https://www.gov.br/mre/pt-br/delbrasaiea/noticias/intervencao-do-brasil-em-ingles-no-debate-sob-o-pilar-ii-nao-proliferacao-na-i-sessao-do-comite-preparatorio-da-xi-conferencia-de-exame-do-tnp-viena-31-7-a-12-8)
- **Locator:** Opening paragraph beginning “We frequently hear...”
- **Period fit:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Official foreign-policy position.
- **Contrary evidence:** Brazil’s nuclear-propulsion program can be viewed as dual-use sensitive even while formally peaceful.
- **Cautions:** Opened page was JS-blocked; the search snippet is the accessible record.
- **Corroboration:** Ministry of Defense treaties page and CTBTO/UN disarmament materials. [Defense treaties page](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/assuntos/relacoes-internacionais/foruns-internacionais-1/tratados-e-regimes)

9. **Lula’s rhetoric opposed nuclear threats**
- **Claim:** In his September 2024 UN General Assembly speech, Lula said the world must not “go back to living with nuclear threats.” This is later-retrospective evidence, but it is consistent with his 2023 anti-escalation posture.  
- **Source:** *President Lula’s speech at the Opening of the General Debate of the 80th UN General Assembly*, MRE, 2024. [Link](https://www.gov.br/mre/en/content-centers/speeches-articles-and-interviews/president-of-the-federative-republic-of-brazil/speeches/president-lula2019s-speech-at-the-opening-of-the-general-debate-of-the-80th-un-general-assembly)
- **Locator:** Lines 43-50 in the opened excerpt.
- **Period fit:** Later retrospective.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential rhetoric.
- **Contrary evidence:** Rhetoric alone does not establish operational restraint.
- **Cautions:** Outside the target year, so use only as retrospective context.
- **Corroboration:** 2023 MRE/Planalto speeches on multilateralism and anti-war themes.

10. **Brazil kept cyber and critical-infrastructure framing**
- **Claim:** The GSI page says the 2023 cyber policy was intended to improve national governance on cyber issues and to address attacks on critical infrastructure. This matters because catastrophic-risk responsibility in 2023 extended beyond nuclear questions into digital infrastructure resilience.
- **Source:** same as record 1.
- **Locator:** Lines 261-262.
- **Period fit:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct executive action.
- **Contrary evidence:** None material within 2023.
- **Cautions:** No implementation results yet.

11. **No evidence of nuclear weapons or weapons deployment**
- **Claim:** All credible official material inspected describes Brazil’s nuclear program as peaceful, safeguarded, and constitutionally non-nuclear-weapon in character. This matters because the primary nuclear-risk question for Lula is not weapons use, but stewardship of a peaceful nuclear sector.
- **Source:** *Tratados e regimes com reflexos para a Defesa*, Ministry of Defense. [Link](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/assuntos/relacoes-internacionais/foruns-internacionais-1/tratados-e-regimes)
- **Locator:** NPT and TPAN sections, especially the text on peaceful use and ABACC.
- **Period fit:** Baseline continuing through 2023.
- **Attribution basis:** Institutional baseline.
- **Contrary evidence:** Dual-use concern from submarine propulsion.
- **Cautions:** Explanatory page, not a treaty record.

12. **CNEN maintained regulatory control**
- **Claim:** CNEN says it regulates about 2,700 radiative installations and 170 nuclear installations, with annual inspection planning and a permanent mandate to keep nuclear/radiological use peaceful. This matters because it is the practical safety-and-custody layer for Brazil’s nuclear posture.
- **Source:** *Radioproteção e Segurança Nuclear*, CNEN. [Link](https://www.gov.br/cnen/pt-br/acesso-a-informacao/transparencia-e-prestacao-de-contas/radioprotecao-e-seguranca-nuclear)
- **Locator:** Paragraph on regulated installations and inspection planning.
- **Period fit:** Inherited baseline with 2023 continuity.
- **Attribution basis:** Institutional baseline.
- **Contrary evidence:** No independent audit here.
- **Cautions:** Regulator self-reporting.

**Information environment and limitations**
- Strongest evidence came from official decrees, treaty/safeguard institutions, and official speeches.
- The nuclear file is unusually sparse in the sense that Brazil does not have a nuclear-weapon program, so there are fewer high-stakes operational decisions to observe directly.
- The most important uncertainty is not whether Brazil had nuclear weapons, but whether its dual-use defense and cyber systems were resourced and implemented effectively.
- A second uncertainty is that many sources are official self-descriptions. They are strong for formal acts and doctrine, weaker for performance.
- For cyber, the policy was formalized in 2023, but implementation quality is still not well measured in the sources inspected.

**Questions for deeper research**
- Did the 2023 cyber policy get funded and staffed enough to matter operationally?
- Did ABACC/IAEA inspections in 2023 detect any new safeguards problems tied to expansion of nuclear installations?
- How much real progress occurred on the nuclear-propulsion submarine beyond budget allocation and ceremonial endorsement?
- Did Lula’s disarmament rhetoric translate into durable foreign-policy commitments beyond speeches?
- Were there any 2023 decisions affecting export controls, proliferation risk, or nuclear supply-chain governance that are not in the first-pass sources?

**Promising leads still needing inspection**
- CTBTO or UNODA records on Brazil’s 2023 disarmament statements and verification posture.
- Official ABACC or IAEA material on 2023 inspection results and safeguards planning.
- Any 2023 CNEN or GSI documents showing actual cyber-security implementation, not just policy creation.
- Any budget execution or audit material for the nuclear-propulsion submarine and related nuclear infrastructure.
- Any 2023 records on export controls or proliferation-risk enforcement beyond the arms decrees.

**Remaining gap**
The main open gap is implementation quality: the 2023 source set is strong on formal acts, doctrine, and rhetoric, but still weak on independent audit of whether the cyber policy, nuclear safeguards, and submarine-program controls actually reduced risk in practice.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

**Orientation**
Lula was Brazil’s president in 2023, but Brazil did not have a nuclear-weapons program to “control” in the usual sense. The nuclear-risk question for this chapter is therefore mostly about peaceful nuclear stewardship, nonproliferation, dual-use defense programs, cyber-risk governance, and whether Lula’s government made Brazil safer or less safe on catastrophic-risk channels. In 2023, the clearest moves were: tightening civilian gun policy, formalizing a national cybersecurity policy, maintaining Brazil’s peaceful nuclear/safeguards posture, continuing the nuclear-propulsion submarine program, and using multilateral rhetoric that opposed escalation and nuclear threats.

**Compact evidence index**
- Lula reversed Bolsonaro-era gun liberalization and shifted inspections toward the Federal Police.
- Lula created a national cybersecurity policy in December 2023.
- Brazil’s official nuclear posture remained peaceful and safeguarded under ABACC/IAEA arrangements.
- The nuclear-propulsion submarine remained a priority and received funding.
- Brazil’s diplomacy remained anti-proliferation oriented in 2023.
- There was no evidence that Lula used nuclear capability to protect aggression or repression.

**Lens disposition**
- **1B.1 Reducing existential risk:** Partially supported.
- **1B.10 Lasting risk posture:** Partially supported.
- **1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** Supported.
- **1B.3 Safe nuclear control:** Supported for peaceful nuclear systems; no nuclear-weapons C2 exists to assess.
- **1B.4 Arms control and inspections:** Supported.
- **1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression:** Supported as negative evidence.
- **1B.6 Preventing proliferation:** Partially supported.
- **1B.7 Independent risk expertise:** Limited evidence.
- **1B.8 Crisis de-escalation:** Limited evidence.
- **1B.9 Other catastrophic risks:** Supported on cyber policy; limited on AI/bio.

**Evidence records**
1. **National cybersecurity policy created**
- **Claim:** Lula signed Decree 11.856/2023 creating Brazil’s National Cybersecurity Policy and National Cybersecurity Committee.  
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest 2023 formal act touching modern catastrophic-risk governance beyond the nuclear file.
- **Source:** GSI, *Presidente Lula assina decreto que institui a Política Nacional de Cibersegurança* (2023-12-27). [Link](https://www.gov.br/gsi/pt-br/centrais-de-conteudo/noticias/2023-1/presidente-lula-assina-decreto-que-institui-a-politica-nacional-de-ciberseguranca)
- **Locator:** Decree reference in the article body.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution:** Direct presidential signature.
- **Caution:** Framework policy, not proof of effective implementation.
- **Supports:** 1B.1, 1B.9, 1B.10

2. **First-day reversal of gun liberalization**
- **Claim:** Lula’s first-day decrees suspended new CAC registrations and began re-regulating civilian firearms access after Bolsonaro-era liberalization.  
- **Why it matters:** This is relevant to domestic catastrophic violence and state control over weapons.
- **Source:** Reuters via UOL, 2023-01-01. [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/01/01/lula-assina-medidas-para-reverter-decretos-de-armas-e-sigilos-de-bolsonaro.htm)
- **Locator:** Opening paragraphs and bullet list.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution:** Direct executive action.
- **Caution:** Reduced access does not remove existing private stockpiles.
- **Supports:** 1B.1, 1B.5, 1B.8, 1B.10

3. **Civilian firearms inspection shifted to Federal Police**
- **Claim:** In July 2023 Lula’s government tightened gun rules and moved inspection from the Army to the Federal Police.  
- **Why it matters:** It reduced military-adjacent oversight of civilian weapons and increased civilian-law-enforcement control.
- **Source:** Reuters via UOL, 2023-07-21. [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/07/21/governo-lula-edita-decreto-que-restringe-acesso-a-armas-e-passa-fiscalizacao-para-a-pf.htm)
- **Locator:** Middle paragraphs on the oversight transfer.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution:** Direct executive regulation.
- **Caution:** Implementation details were still evolving.
- **Supports:** 1B.1, 1B.5, 1B.8, 1B.10

4. **Peaceful nuclear doctrine**
- **Claim:** Brazil’s official defense material describes the nuclear sector as exclusively peaceful, with the nuclear-propulsion submarine permitted under the IAEA arrangement and weapons remaining conventional.  
- **Why it matters:** Establishes the baseline nuclear posture under Lula.
- **Source:** Ministry of Defense, *Setor nuclear*. [Link](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/assuntos/industria-de-defesa/ciencia-e-tecnologia/setores-estrategicos/setor-nuclear)
- **Locator:** Paragraphs stating peaceful use, IAEA permission, and conventional armaments.
- **Period:** Inherited baseline continuing in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Institutional baseline, not uniquely Lula-created.
- **Caution:** Official self-description; dual-use sensitivity remains.
- **Supports:** 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.5, 1B.6

5. **ABACC safeguards activity**
- **Claim:** ABACC said the liaison committee reviewed 2022 inspection results and discussed safeguards challenges from the expansion of nuclear installations.  
- **Why it matters:** Shows the safeguards system was active and thinking about future risk.
- **Source:** ABACC, *21st Meeting of the Liaison Committee...* (2023-10-09). [Link](https://www.abacc.org.br/en/news/21st-meeting-liaison-committee-lc-quadripartite-safeguards-agreement/)
- **Locator:** Lines 63-64.
- **Period:** Target year, with 2022 reference.
- **Attribution:** Institutional context under Lula.
- **Caution:** Not an independent audit of outcomes.
- **Supports:** 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.7, 1B.10

6. **Itaguaí nuclear-submarine endorsement**
- **Claim:** After visiting Itaguaí in March 2023, Lula publicly backed the nuclear-propulsion submarine program and said he expected it to advance.  
- **Why it matters:** Confirms presidential support for the main dual-use nuclear-adjacent defense program.
- **Source:** Presidency transcript, 2023-04-03. [Link](https://www.gov.br/planalto/pt-br/acompanhe-o-planalto/entrevistas/fala-do-presidente-da-republica-luiz-inacio-lula-da-silva-em-entrevista-coletiva-apos-visita-ao-complexo-naval-do-itaguai)
- **Locator:** Q&A segment on the submarine.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution:** Direct presidential endorsement.
- **Caution:** Endorsement does not prove execution quality.
- **Supports:** 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.10

7. **Submarine funding in Novo PAC**
- **Claim:** The 2023 Novo PAC allocated R$664 million to the nuclear-propulsion submarine line and R$53 billion overall to defense projects.  
- **Why it matters:** Budget priorities are a core indicator of real commitment.
- **Source:** Ministry of Defense, 2023-08-11. [Link](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/ajuste/com-novo-pac-defesa-investira-r-53-bilhoes-em-tecnologias-estrategicas-que-garantem-a-soberania-nacional/)
- **Locator:** Paragraph on the submarine and investment table.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution:** Government budget choice.
- **Caution:** Allocation is not the same as delivered capability.
- **Supports:** 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.10

8. **Brazil’s NPT nonproliferation posture**
- **Claim:** Brazil’s August 2023 NPT PrepCom intervention said disarmament and nonproliferation should not be treated as a false equivalence and stressed the underperformance of disarmament obligations.  
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest official anti-proliferation rhetoric in the target year.
- **Source:** Ministry of Foreign Affairs, 2023-08-07. [Link](https://www.gov.br/mre/pt-br/delbrasaiea/noticias/intervencao-do-brasil-em-ingles-no-debate-sob-o-pilar-ii-nao-proliferacao-na-i-sessao-do-comite-preparatorio-da-xi-conferencia-de-exame-do-tnp-viena-31-7-a-12-8)
- **Locator:** Opening paragraph.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution:** Official Brazilian foreign-policy position.
- **Caution:** Diplomatic language does not prove domestic execution.
- **Supports:** 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.8

9. **Anti-nuclear-threat rhetoric, later confirmation**
- **Claim:** Lula later said the world should not return to living with nuclear threats, consistent with his 2023 posture.  
- **Why it matters:** Useful as retrospective confirmation of anti-escalation rhetoric.
- **Source:** MRE UNGA speech page, 2025. [Link](https://www.gov.br/mre/en/content-centers/speeches-articles-and-interviews/president-of-the-federative-republic-of-brazil/speeches/president-lula2019s-speech-at-the-opening-of-the-general-debate-of-the-80th-un-general-assembly)
- **Locator:** Lines 43-50.
- **Period:** Later retrospective.
- **Attribution:** Direct presidential rhetoric.
- **Caution:** Outside the target year.
- **Supports:** 1B.2, 1B.8, 1B.10

10. **Multilateral anti-war framing**
- **Claim:** Lula’s COP28 speech framed the period as one of war and global human challenge, consistent with anti-escalation rhetoric.  
- **Why it matters:** Context for how Lula talked about conflict and catastrophe in 2023.
- **Source:** MMA, COP28 speech page, 2023-12-01. [Link](https://www.gov.br/mma/pt-br/discurso-do-presidente-lula-no-segmento-de-alto-nivel-da-cop-28)
- **Locator:** Opening paragraphs.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution:** Direct presidential rhetoric.
- **Caution:** General anti-war language, not nuclear-specific.
- **Supports:** 1B.2, 1B.8

11. **CNEN regulatory capacity**
- **Claim:** CNEN says it regulates about 2,700 radiative installations and 170 nuclear installations through annual inspection planning and a peaceful-use mandate.  
- **Why it matters:** Gives the practical safety-and-custody baseline for Brazil’s nuclear sector.
- **Source:** CNEN, *Radioproteção e Segurança Nuclear*. [Link](https://www.gov.br/cnen/pt-br/acesso-a-informacao/transparencia-e-prestacao-de-contas/radioprotecao-e-seguranca-nuclear)
- **Locator:** Paragraph on regulated installations and inspections.
- **Period:** Baseline continuing in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Institutional baseline.
- **Caution:** Self-reported regulator capacity.
- **Supports:** 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.10

**Information environment**
- Strong on formal acts, speeches, and institutional doctrine.
- Weak on independent auditing of implementation results.
- No evidence of nuclear-weapons command issues because Brazil had none.
- The main uncertainty is whether policy changes translated into measurable risk reduction.

**Questions for deeper research**
- Did the cyber policy receive real funding and operational follow-through?
- Did ABACC/IAEA identify any 2023 safeguards weakness tied to expanding nuclear installations?
- How much of the submarine program advanced beyond budget and rhetoric?
- Were there any 2023 export-control or proliferation-control decisions beyond firearms policy?
- Did Lula’s anti-escalation rhetoric affect actual foreign-policy practice in crises?

**Corroborating sources**
- [Planalto D11856](https://www.presidencia.gov.br/ccivil_03/_ato2023-2026/2023/decreto/d11856.htm)
- [Planalto D11615](https://www.planalto.gov.br/ccivil_03/_ato2023-2026/2023/decreto/d11615.htm)
- [ABACC liaison committee report](https://www.abacc.org.br/en/news/21st-meeting-liaison-committee-lc-quadripartite-safeguards-agreement/)
- [Defense nuclear sector page](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/assuntos/industria-de-defesa/ciencia-e-tecnologia/setores-estrategicos/setor-nuclear)
- [CNEN safeguards page](https://www.gov.br/cnen/pt-br/acesso-a-informacao/transparencia-e-prestacao-de-contas/radioprotecao-e-seguranca-nuclear)
- [MRE NPT intervention](https://www.gov.br/mre/pt-br/delbrasaiea/noticias/intervencao-do-brasil-em-ingles-no-debate-sob-o-pilar-ii-nao-proliferacao-na-i-sessao-do-comite-preparatorio-da-xi-conferencia-de-exame-do-tnp-viena-31-7-a-12-8)

**Promising leads**
- CTBTO records on Brazil’s verification posture.
- IAEA/ABACC material on 2023 inspection results.
- Budget execution for the nuclear-propulsion submarine.
- Any 2023 official material on AI, biosecurity, or cyber implementation beyond the decree.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

**Orientation**
Brazil under Lula in 2023 was not an active belligerent state. The relevant peace-and-security question is whether the government restrained escalation, used lawful channels, protected civilians, and avoided coercive or proxy behavior. The record is strongest on diplomacy and crisis management: Brazil repeatedly called for ceasefires, negotiations, and multilateral solutions in Ukraine and Gaza; it rejected military solutions; it repatriated Brazilians from Israel and Gaza; and it maintained a normalizing regional posture toward Venezuela. The main limits were inherited constraints on Brazil’s ability to shape foreign conflicts, the reality that its diplomacy was declaratory rather than coercive, and the absence of a major war of choice to judge.

**Compact evidence index**
- Brazil consistently rejected military solutions in Ukraine and called for mediation, dialogue, and negotiated settlement.
- Brazil condemned the 2022 Russian invasion and kept its 2023 posture inside UN Charter language.
- Brazil did not supply war materiel to Ukraine; Lula explicitly rejected sending ammunition.
- Brazil managed the Israel-Gaza crisis mainly through evacuation, repatriation, and humanitarian-corridor diplomacy.
- Lula condemned Hamas attacks and also condemned Israel’s Gaza response as excessive and unlawful.
- Brazil continued normalization with Venezuela through diplomatic and border-management mechanisms.
- There is no evidence in the inspected record that Lula initiated or prolonged an interstate war, supported proxies in a war, or used force outside lawful security and evacuation contexts.

**Lens disposition**
- **2B.1 Peaceful alternatives:** Supported.
- **2B.10 Lasting international peace:** Partially supported.
- **2B.2 Starting or prolonging war:** Supported as negative evidence.
- **2B.3 Honest justification for force:** Supported.
- **2B.4 Civilian and prisoner protection:** Partially supported.
- **2B.5 Control of proxies and arms:** Mostly no direct exposure in 2023; limited evidence.
- **2B.6 Scrutiny of war claims:** Supported through public diplomacy and open debate, but not strongly through domestic oversight records.
- **2B.7 Ceasefires and settlements:** Supported.
- **2B.8 Responsible military resources:** Limited evidence.
- **2B.9 Accountability and remedy:** Partially supported, mainly through evacuation/repatriation and public legal framing.

**Evidence records**

1. **Brazil pursued negotiation first in Ukraine**
- **Claim:** In 2023 Lula repeatedly said Brazil’s role was to help build peace in Ukraine through countries not directly involved in the war, and that negotiation should remain open. This matters because it is the clearest evidence of restraint-oriented conflict policy.
- **Source:** Reuters via UOL, 2023-02-24, *Lula defende que países não envolvidos em guerra na Ucrânia encaminhem negociação de paz*. [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/02/24/lula-defende-que-paises-nao-envolvidos-na-guerra-na-ucrania-encaminhem-negociacao-de-paz.htm)
- **Locator:** Reuters report quoting Lula on a group of non-involved countries promoting negotiations.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential rhetoric and foreign-policy direction.
- **Contrary evidence:** Critics argued this approach risked false equivalence between aggressor and victim; the record does not show a concrete peace plan accepted by both sides.
- **Cautions:** This is rhetoric and diplomacy, not a settlement outcome.
- **Corroboration:** MRE UNSC speech on 2023-09-21; Reuters on 2023-04-26; Reuters on 2023-08-02.
- **Supports:** 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.7, 2B.10

2. **Brazil refused to send ammunition to Ukraine**
- **Claim:** Lula said Brazil would not send ammunition for use in the Ukraine war and argued the country should not be even indirectly involved. This matters because it is direct evidence against proxy support or escalation.
- **Source:** Reuters via UOL, 2023-01-30, *Lula diz que Brasil não vai enviar munições à Ucrânia e cobra ação da China para acordo de paz*. [Link](https://noticias.uol.com.br/ultimas-noticias/reuters/2023/01/30/lula-diz-que-china-precisa-por-a-mao-na-massa-em-conversas-de-paz-na-ucrania-e-quer-falar-com-xi.htm)
- **Locator:** Early paragraphs and Lula’s quoted statement.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct executive decision and public explanation.
- **Contrary evidence:** None in the inspected material. It is consistent with Brazil’s broader restraint posture.
- **Cautions:** One shipment decision does not cover all indirect support channels.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters on 2023-04-26 and MRE on 2023-09-21.
- **Supports:** 2B.2, 2B.5, 2B.7, 2B.8

3. **Brazil condemned the Russian invasion while keeping diplomatic channels open**
- **Claim:** Lula said Brazil condemned Russia’s invasion of Ukraine but also wanted diplomatic channels open and a ceasefire. This matters because it shows a balanced public justification: condemnation of aggression without military escalation.
- **Source:** Reuters via UOL, 2023-04-26, *Lula pede que caminhos diplomáticos em guerra Rússia-Ucrânia não sejam fechados*. [Link](https://economia.uol.com.br/noticias/reuters/2023/04/26/lula-pede-que-caminhos-diplomaticos-em-guerra-russia-ucrania-nao-sejam-fechados.htm)
- **Locator:** Reuters quotation from Lula’s remarks in Spain.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential rhetoric.
- **Contrary evidence:** Some observers saw the approach as too permissive toward Russia; the source itself shows no endorsement of Russian aggression.
- **Cautions:** Rhetorical posture, not operational enforcement.
- **Corroboration:** MRE UNSC speech on 2023-09-21.
- **Supports:** 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.7, 2B.10

4. **Brazil’s UN Security Council posture on Ukraine favored de-escalation**
- **Claim:** At the UN Security Council in September 2023, Brazil said there was no military solution in Ukraine, called for de-escalation and dialogue, and said Brazil was prepared to do its part. This matters because it is the clearest formal diplomatic record of restraint and lawful-security framing.
- **Source:** Ministry of Foreign Affairs, 2023-09-21, UNSC open debate speech by Mauro Vieira. [Link](https://www.gov.br/mre/pt-br/delbrasonu/the-permanent-mission-of-brazil-to-the-un/statements/2023/september-2023/discurso-do-ministro-de-estado-das-relacoes-exteriores-embaixador-mauro-luiz-iecker-vieira-em-debate-aberto-do-conselho-de-seguranca-sobre-a-defesa-dos-propositos-e-principios-da-carta-das-nacoes-unidas-atraves-do-multilateralismo-efetivo-manutencao-de)
- **Locator:** Lines 353-371, especially 363-370.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Official foreign-policy statement under Lula’s government.
- **Contrary evidence:** The statement supports Ukraine negotiation but does not prove that Brazil materially changed the conflict.
- **Cautions:** Diplomatic language is designed to balance principles and does not itself establish results.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters on 2023-02-24, 2023-04-26, 2023-08-02, and 2023-09-21.
- **Supports:** 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.7, 2B.10

5. **Brazil maintained peace-through-diplomacy on Ukraine at the UN**
- **Claim:** Brazil emphasized that the war had global food and energy consequences, supported ceasefire and mediation, and said both parties should be heard. This matters because it shows a durable, not one-off, restraint posture.
- **Source:** same MRE UNSC speech as above. [Link](https://www.gov.br/mre/pt-br/delbrasonu/the-permanent-mission-of-brazil-to-the-un/statements/2023/september-2023/discurso-do-ministro-de-estado-das-relacoes-exteriores-embaixador-mauro-luiz-iecker-vieira-em-debate-aberto-do-conselho-de-seguranca-sobre-a-defesa-dos-propositos-e-principios-da-carta-das-nacoes-unidas-atraves-do-multilateralismo-efetivo-manutencao-de)
- **Locator:** Lines 357-371.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Official diplomatic doctrine.
- **Contrary evidence:** No concrete peace settlement followed in 2023.
- **Cautions:** This is posture, not outcome.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters reporting on Lula’s statements in February, April, August, and September 2023.
- **Supports:** 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10

6. **Brazil repatriated its nationals from the Israel-Gaza war zone**
- **Claim:** After the October 7 attacks and Israel’s response, Brazil organized a government-led evacuation/repatriation operation for citizens in Israel and the Palestinian territories. This matters because it is a lawful-security response to conflict exposure, not a military escalation.
- **Source:** Ministry of Health, 2023-10-14, *Ministério da Saúde envia profissionais da Força Nacional do SUS...* [Link](https://www.gov.br/saude/pt-br/assuntos/noticias/2023/outubro/ministerio-da-saude-envia-profissionais-da-forca-nacional-do-sus-para-receber-brasileiros-repatriados-de-israel)
- **Locator:** Lines 299-304.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct implementation “por determinação do Presidente da República.”
- **Contrary evidence:** Evacuation policy does not address the underlying war.
- **Cautions:** The page is an operational update, not a casualty or compliance audit.
- **Corroboration:** Ministry of Defense repatriation updates and MRE notices.
- **Supports:** 2B.4, 2B.9, 2B.10

7. **Brazil’s Gaza posture combined condemnation of Hamas with condemnation of Israeli conduct**
- **Claim:** In October 2023 Lula condemned Hamas as terrorism and simultaneously condemned Israel’s response in Gaza as “insane.” This matters because it shows an attempt to justify force claims in public-law language rather than nationalist escalation.
- **Source:** Reuters via UOL, 2023-10-20, *Lula diz que Hamas praticou atos de "terrorismo" e reação de Israel em Gaza é "insana"*. [Link](https://economia.uol.com.br/noticias/reuters/2023/10/20/lula-diz-que-hamas-praticou-atos-de-terrorismo-e-reacao-de-israel-em-gaza-e-insana.htm)
- **Locator:** Lead paragraphs and quoted remarks.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential rhetoric.
- **Contrary evidence:** Critics argued the framing was too harsh on Israel or too ambiguous on Hamas; the source shows both condemnations.
- **Cautions:** Rhetorical and politically controversial; not a legal finding.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters via UOL on 2023-10-12, 2023-11-13, 2023-12-04.
- **Supports:** 2B.3, 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.7, 2B.9

8. **Brazil pushed for humanitarian corridors and civilian evacuation in Gaza**
- **Claim:** Lula asked Israel’s president for a humanitarian corridor from Gaza and the Brazilian government used diplomatic and military assets to repatriate Brazilian nationals. This matters because it is an example of civilian protection during conflict.
- **Source:** Reuters via UOL, 2023-10-12, *Lula faz apelo por corredor humanitário para saída de Gaza em telefonema com presidente de Israel*. [Link](https://economia.uol.com.br/noticias/reuters/2023/10/12/lula-faz-apelo-por-corredor-humanitario-para-saida-de-gaza-em-telefonema-com-presidente-de-israel.amp.htm)
- **Locator:** Reported phone call and Lula’s quoted appeal.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential initiative and diplomacy.
- **Contrary evidence:** Humanitarian corridors depend on other parties’ consent.
- **Cautions:** Wire-reporting based on Lula’s post-call summary and a source with knowledge of the conversation.
- **Corroboration:** MRE and Ministry of Defense repatriation updates.
- **Supports:** 2B.1, 2B.4, 2B.7, 2B.9

9. **Brazil later called the Israel-Gaza war genocide and backed two states**
- **Claim:** In December 2023 Lula said the only solution was two states and criticized the UN’s failure to act. This matters because it shows continued diplomatic pressure for settlement, even amid harsh rhetoric.
- **Source:** Reuters via UOL, 2023-12-04, *Lula defende solução de dois Estados para conflito em Gaza e critica atuação da ONU*. [Link](https://economia.uol.com.br/noticias/reuters/2023/12/04/lula-defende-solucao-de-dois-estados-para-conflito-em-gaza-e-critica-atuacao-da-onu.htm)
- **Locator:** Lead paragraph and quoted remarks.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential rhetoric.
- **Contrary evidence:** The language was severe and politically contested, but it did not advocate escalation.
- **Cautions:** Controversial characterizations can blur legal and political judgments.
- **Corroboration:** MRE Middle East Security Council statement from 2023-10-24.
- **Supports:** 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.7, 2B.10

10. **Brazil publicly favored ceasefire and two-state diplomacy at the UN**
- **Claim:** At the Security Council debate on the Middle East, Brazil urged a ceasefire, humanitarian corridor, and two-state solution, and told states to avoid politicization. This matters because it is a clear lawful-security and peace-process record.
- **Source:** MRE, 2023-10-24, *Address by Minister Mauro Vieira at the Security Council debate on the Middle East*. [Link](https://www.gov.br/mre/en/content-centers/speeches-articles-and-interviews/minister-of-foreign-affairs/speeches/mauro-vieira-2023/address-by-minister-mauro-vieira-at-the-security-council-debate-on-the-middle-east-new-york-october-24-2023)
- **Locator:** Search result excerpt lines on calm, restraint, peace, cease-fire, humanitarian corridor, and two-state solution.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Official diplomatic statement.
- **Contrary evidence:** This did not produce a negotiated settlement in 2023.
- **Cautions:** The opened page was JS-blocked; the search excerpt is the usable record.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters on 2023-10-12, 2023-10-20, 2023-12-04.
- **Supports:** 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10

11. **Brazil did not treat Israel-Gaza as a military commitment**
- **Claim:** The public record shows Brazil’s involvement was repatriation, diplomatic appeals, and humanitarian support, not armed participation. This matters because it is the best available negative evidence on war prolongation or proxy support.
- **Source:** Ministry of Defense repatriation notice, 2023-10-08, *Em coordenação com o Itamaraty, Defesa inicia missão para repatriação de brasileiros em Israel*. [Link](https://www.gov.br/defesa/pt-br/centrais-de-conteudo/noticias/em-coordenacao-com-o-itamaraty-defesa-inicia-missao-para-repatriacao-de-brasileiros-em-israel)
- **Locator:** Opening paragraphs describing FAB aircraft made available for repatriation.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct executive decision.
- **Contrary evidence:** None in the inspected material.
- **Cautions:** This covers a rescue operation, not the full foreign-policy record.
- **Corroboration:** Ministry of Health repatriation follow-up.
- **Supports:** 2B.2, 2B.4, 2B.8, 2B.9

12. **Brazil normalized relations with Venezuela through civilian border mechanisms**
- **Claim:** In June 2023 Brazil and Venezuela agreed to restart bilateral commercial and sanitary mechanisms and conduct joint inspections at Boa Vista. This matters because it shows restraint, legalization, and avoidance of coercive escalation in a sensitive regional relationship.
- **Source:** MRE, 2023-06-20, *Agricultural Trade with Venezuela - Joint Statement...* [Link](https://www.gov.br/mre/en/contact-us/press-area/press-releases/agricultural-trade-with-venezuela-joint-statement-by-the-ministry-of-foreign-relations-and-the-ministry-of-agriculture-and-livestock)
- **Locator:** Lines 162-164.
- **Period:** Target period.
- **Attribution basis:** Official bilateral diplomacy.
- **Contrary evidence:** This is trade and border administration, not a peace treaty or security settlement.
- **Cautions:** Useful as restraint evidence, but not directly about armed conflict.
- **Corroboration:** Same source and MRE regional-relations pages.
- **Supports:** 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10

**Information environment and limitations**
- The record is strong on diplomatic intent and crisis-management actions.
- It is weaker on hard outcomes, because Brazil was mostly not a direct combatant and 2023 had no Brazil-initiated war to audit.
- Official sources are abundant but self-descriptive; Reuters adds useful independent confirmation.
- Some of the most consequential evidence concerns “what Brazil did not do” - no weapons transfer to Ukraine, no military involvement in Gaza or Venezuela - which is inherently harder to prove exhaustively.
- Public scrutiny was relatively open, especially through Reuters, MRE, and ministry notices.

**Questions for deeper research**
- Did Brazil’s mediation role on Ukraine have any measurable effect beyond rhetoric and participation in talks?
- Did the Gaza repatriation operation cover all requested nationals, and how quickly?
- Were there any covert or indirect arms/support channels that the inspected record misses?
- Did Brazil’s Venezuela normalization have any security consequences on the border beyond trade facilitation?
- How should Lula’s sharply critical Gaza rhetoric be weighed against his repeated endorsement of humanitarian law and two-state diplomacy?

**Promising leads still needing inspection**
- UN Security Council and General Assembly transcripts for Lula or Mauro Vieira beyond the excerpts surfaced here.
- Official MRE pages on Ukraine and Middle East peace/security categories.
- Any formal Brazilian role in Colombia/ELN peace facilitation in 2023.
- Any TCU or parliamentary oversight on foreign-policy or military-resource use relevant to conflict exposure.

- **Rejected sources:** None in this pass.
- **Blocked sources:** None in this pass.
